\subsection{How to read a card}

\textbf{How this failure starts} states what the team was legitimately trying to achieve — no card here describes carelessness — then names the shape that gets built and the mechanism by which it fails, which is what you need in order to recognize the same failure in an unfamiliar system. \textbf{What it looks like in practice} is a concrete instance, drawn from the Harborlight mortgage-evidence scenario where it fits and from another domain where that better shows transfer. \textbf{How to replace it} gives the fix and its migration path, and \textbf{How to know the repair worked} gives the signals that tell you whether you still have the problem and the honest cost of the correction. \textbf{Prevented in / Built in} names the chapter that already prevents the failure, or previews it, and the later chapter that builds its replacement.\par

The cost sentence in each card matters. Every correction in this catalog costs something — latency, flexibility, or engineering time — and applying all sixteen to a read-only internal assistant would be malpractice. Correct in order of the blast radius of the effects involved. Start with AP-04, AP-05, and AP-06: they produce duplicate payments, duplicate records, and unauthorized effects while looking like ordinary diligence. IDs are stable identifiers; cards are ordered by the boundary they violate.\par

\section{Reasoning and control flow}

These two failures share a root cause: the model is allowed to decide when to stop. Once stopping is a model decision, termination, budget, and blast radius all become probabilistic properties of a text generator, and no amount of prompt tuning makes them deterministic again. The correction in both cases is to move the stopping decision into code that the model cannot reach.\par

\subsection{AP-01 — Autonomous monolith}

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/anti-before-after.pdf}{Figure 17-1 — Autonomous monolith versus governed agentic architecture}{anti-before-after}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} A prototype that worked convincingly in a notebook is promoted toward production. It mixes reasoning, run state, credentials, tool invocation, retries, approval, and its own completion signal in a single process, because nothing in the prototype phase forced those concerns apart. The tempting shortcut is “One unconstrained agent owns end-to-end control. The loop decides what to do, does it, and then declares the outcome.” It breaks down because probabilistic behavior becomes the control logic. There is no boundary at which a failure can be isolated, so a flawed reasoning step and a failed database write are indistinguishable from outside the process. Run state lives in memory, so a restart loses work that has already had external effects. Nothing can be replayed, because nothing durable was recorded — the transcript is a narrative, not a ledger.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} Mortgage evidence review restarts after a routine deploy and sends the same document request twice, because the only record of the first request was the agent's conversation history.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} A bounded runtime inside a durable workflow, with policy, tool and effect, and acceptance services each owning their decision. Migrate in steps: Monolith → direct tools → “done” becomes workflow → semantic nodes → governed effects → acceptance.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track protected calls that bypass the gateway, work lost per restart, false-completion rate, and the number of distinct responsibilities living in one process. The cost: decomposition adds interfaces, deployment surface, and latency. Decompose around the highest-risk effects first; a read-only assistant does not need the full split.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Prevented in Chapters 1, 3, and 15.\par

\subsection{AP-11 — Unbounded loops and recursive delegation}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Work varies in complexity, and a fixed step count either truncates hard cases or wastes budget on easy ones, so the team lets the agent iterate and delegate as needed. The tempting shortcut is “Agents iterate and spawn children with per-call limits but no global ceiling. Each individual decision looks reasonable.” It breaks down because local limits do not compose. A depth limit of five with a fan-out of five is 3,125 leaf tasks, and each child inherits a fresh budget rather than a share of the parent's. Delegation graphs become cyclic when two specialists can each call the other, and no participant can detect the cycle from inside it. Queue saturation then degrades unrelated tenants, which is how one runaway research task becomes a platform incident.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} A research supervisor spawns fact-checkers that themselves spawn fact-checkers; the run consumes a day of tenant quota before a human notices, and every individual agent behaved exactly as instructed.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} Global budgets that decrease monotonically across delegation, an acyclic ownership graph, and explicit depth, fan-out, and no-progress limits enforced by the runtime rather than by instructions. Migrate in steps: Recursion becomes a runtime-governed task graph.\par

\BookNeedspace{293.6pt}

\BookSnake · AP-11 and its correction: delegation draws on one budget\par\nopagebreak

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
@dataclass
class Budget:
    tokens: int
    depth: int
    ancestors: frozenset[str] = frozenset()

    def withdraw(self, tokens: int, owner: str) -> "Budget":
        """Fund a child from this budget; a child never holds more than its parent."""
        if self.depth <= 0:
            raise DelegationDepthExceeded(owner)
        if owner in self.ancestors:              # acyclic ownership
            raise DelegationCycle(owner)
        if tokens > self.tokens:
            raise BudgetExhausted(owner)
        self.tokens -= tokens                    # the parent's balance falls
        return Budget(tokens, self.depth - 1, self.ancestors | {owner})


# Anti-pattern: every child starts fresh, so the ceiling is depth x fan-out.
def delegate_naive(task: Task, worker: Worker) -> object:
    return worker.run(task, Budget(tokens=20_000, depth=3))   # reset on every hop


# Correction: delegation is a withdrawal from the parent's remaining budget,
# so the whole subtree is bounded by the root's admission.
def delegate(task: Task, worker: Worker, parent: Budget) -> object:
    child = parent.withdraw(task.estimate, owner=task.owner)
    return worker.run(task, child)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track delegation depth and fan-out distributions, repeated state signatures, no-progress step count, and budget-exhaustion rate by tenant. The cost: caps truncate genuinely hard work. Set them from observed distributions rather than intuition, and make exhaustion a visible, escalatable terminal rather than a silent stop.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Previewed in Chapter 10; built in Chapters 18 and 28.\par

\section{Context, knowledge and memory}

Everything an agent believes arrives through one of these three channels, and all three fail the same way: a convenient representation is mistaken for an authoritative one. A transcript is convenient. A similarity hit is convenient. A remembered fact is convenient. None of them is a system of record, and treating any of them as one moves business truth into a store with no owner, no schema, and no audit.\par

\subsection{AP-02 — Prompt as database}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Business state has to survive across turns, restarts, and handoffs, and the conversation already contains it. The tempting shortcut is “Chat history, or a rolling summary of it, is treated as the authoritative record of what has happened.” It breaks down because every mechanism that keeps a context window viable is lossy. Truncation drops the oldest facts, which are often the decisions; summarization paraphrases, and a paraphrase of “legal approved with conditions” is not a legal approval. There is no concurrency control, so two turns that both update a value silently diverge. There is no schema, so nothing detects that a field went missing. And there is no audit trail an investigator can read.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} A contract renewal forgets that legal approved with conditions, because the approval was three summarization rounds ago and the summary kept the outcome but dropped the conditions.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} Canonical typed state in a service that owns it, with the prompt receiving a projection built fresh from that state on every step. Migrate in steps: Transcript-as-state becomes State Service → snapshot → prompt.\par

\BookNeedspace{284.4pt}

\BookSnake · AP-02 and its correction: typed state projected into context\par\nopagebreak

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
# Anti-pattern: the transcript is the record, so whatever survives
# compaction is what the system believes.
def remember_naive(messages: list[str]) -> list[str]:
    messages.append("Legal approved, with an indemnity cap and 90-day notice.")
    messages.append("Legal approved.")
    return summarize(messages, keep=1)         # the conditions are gone


# Correction: the decision is a typed record; the prompt is a projection of it.
# Compaction can lose a sentence without losing the fact.
def remember(state: StateStore) -> list[str]:
    state.record(
        DecisionRecord(
            authority="legal",
            disposition="approved_with_conditions",
            conditions=("indemnity_cap_2m", "termination_notice_90d"),
        ),
        expected_version=state.version,        # optimistic concurrency
    )
    return project_context(state.snapshot())


def project_context(snapshot: tuple[DecisionRecord, ...]) -> list[str]:
    """Build the prompt's state section fresh from the store, every step."""
    return [
        f"{d.authority}: {d.disposition} ({', '.join(d.conditions)})" for d in snapshot
    ]
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track facts lost across compaction boundaries, disagreement between summary and store, and state mismatches discovered on recovery. The cost: typed state requires schema design and migration discipline. It lands early and visibly, which is precisely why it gets deferred.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Prevented in Chapters 6 and 15.\par

\subsection{AP-09 — Vector store as source of truth}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Relevant knowledge has to be found across a corpus too large to pass into a prompt. The tempting shortcut is “A similarity hit is treated as a current, authorized, authoritative fact.” It breaks down because similarity measures resemblance, not currency, authority, or permission. An index contains what was ingested, including superseded policies, duplicated drafts, documents the caller may not read, and anything an attacker managed to insert. Nothing in a cosine score distinguishes the approved policy from its withdrawn predecessor, and a confident citation of the wrong document is more damaging than no citation at all.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} An obsolete policy chunk applies a superseded spending limit, and the answer cites the document by name, which is what makes the reviewer believe it.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} Governed sources and snapshots hold authority, and the snapshot is a pinned knowledge release (Chapter 5); the index is a derived locator whose hits are verified for ACL, freshness, and supersession before the content reaches the model. Migrate in steps: Vector hit → action becomes source + ACL + snapshot → evidence → decision.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track evidence age at decision time, source disagreement rate, ACL mismatches at retrieval, and citations pointing at superseded revisions. The cost: authoritative lookup adds latency to every retrieval. Apply it in proportion to what the answer will be used for; drafting assistance and payment authorization do not need the same guarantee.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Prevented in Chapter 5.\par

\subsection{AP-13 — Memory write by default}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Personalization is genuinely valuable, and the cheapest way to get it is to remember everything. The tempting shortcut is “Every interaction, or a summary of it, becomes a durable memory record.” It breaks down because writing by default means writing without provenance, purpose, or verification. Secrets get persisted. Model errors get persisted and then retrieved as established fact, which is how one hallucination becomes a standing belief. Injected instructions from a document become durable instructions. And because nothing recorded why a memory was written, nothing can decide when it should expire or who may delete it — which turns a retention obligation into an archaeology project.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} A malicious mortgage document is processed, and the bank account it contains is stored as an applicant fact. Every subsequent run retrieves it as if it were verified evidence for the mortgage case.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} Memory writes are candidates, not side effects: purpose-bound, carrying provenance, verified before promotion, scoped to a tenant and subject, with a TTL and a deletion path. Migrate in steps: Transcript → memory becomes candidate → gate → typed record.\par

\BookNeedspace{183.2pt}

\BookSnake · AP-13 and its correction: a memory write passes a gate\par\nopagebreak

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
# Anti-pattern: whatever the run produced becomes durable memory, with no
# provenance, no purpose, and no way to expire.
def remember_naive(memory: MemoryStore, subject: str, summary: str) -> None:
    memory.records.append((subject, summary))


# Correction: a write is a proposal that must survive a gate. Untrusted
# provenance cannot become durable memory, and every record carries the
# purpose that justifies its retention.
def remember(memory: MemoryStore, gate: MemoryGate,
             candidate: MemoryCandidate, today: date) -> bool:
    if gate.admits(candidate):        # source trust, purpose, no secrets, expiry
        memory.records.append(candidate.as_record(today))
        return True
    memory.rejected.append((candidate, gate.reason))   # audited, not silent
    return False
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track sensitive-data writes caught by DLP, retrieval of stale records, correction and deletion rates, and incidents traceable to a single memory record. The cost: a strict write path reduces the perceived magic of personalization and requires someone to own purpose definitions. The alternative is an unowned database of unverified claims about your customers.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Prevented in Chapter 6.\par

\section{Security and delegated authority}

The three cards here are the difference between \emph{can} and \emph{may}. An agent that can reach a resource, can see a tool, or can produce a plausible safety score has demonstrated capability, not permission. Each card is a case where capability was allowed to stand in for a policy decision, and in each the correction is the same: make the authorization decision explicitly, in code, at the moment of use.\par

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/anti-ambient-authority.pdf}{Figure 17-2 — Ambient authority versus a narrowed capability envelope and a short-lived credential lease}{anti-ambient-authority}

\subsection{AP-03 — Ambient authority}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Agents need access to enterprise systems, and provisioning narrow credentials for every task and tenant is laborious. The tempting shortcut is “Workers receive broad, long-lived, reusable credentials — often a service account that predates the agent and was already over-scoped.” It breaks down because the credential's scope, not the task's need, defines the blast radius. Any compromise — prompt injection, a malicious tool, a leaked log — yields durable authority over everything the credential can reach. Because the same credential serves every run, nothing in the audit trail distinguishes which subject's work caused which effect, so attribution fails exactly when it is needed. And a credential that never expires cannot be revoked faster than someone notices.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} The mortgage evidence agent runs under a service account that can export every applicant record; the evidence-review task needs one case. The account was reused because it already existed.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} Preserve both subject and workload identity, derive a capability envelope by intersection, and mint a short-lived, audience-bound credential lease at the moment of use. Migrate in steps: Broad token becomes policy → capability envelope → credential lease → enforcement at the resource.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track credential scope and age distributions, granted-but-unused privilege, scope-expansion attempts, and cross-tenant access denials. The cost: fine-grained policy adds design effort and a policy-decision hop per call. The hop is cacheable; the design effort is not, and it is the part teams underestimate.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Previewed in Chapters 1 and 3; built in Chapter 21.\par

\subsection{AP-10 — LLM judge as security gate}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Some content is genuinely ambiguous, and a model is better than a regular expression at recognizing nuance. The tempting shortcut is “A model-produced score alone authorizes a protected action — if risk\_score < 0.3: execute().” It breaks down because a judge is an injectable, nondeterministic component being asked to make a binding decision. The same input can score differently across runs, so the gate is not reproducible and an incident cannot be explained. The content being judged can address the judge directly, which makes the attacker a participant in their own authorization. And judge behavior drifts with model updates, silently moving the threshold without any release event.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} The judge classifies an unusually large payment as low risk because the request text explains, persuasively, why it is routine.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} A deterministic policy and invariant gate makes the decision. The judge triages, prioritizes, and supplies evidence for a human — it never holds the authorization. Migrate in steps: score < threshold → execute becomes policy → optional judge → human escalation.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track disagreement rate across repeated runs of identical input, gate bypass attempts, false negatives found downstream, and human override frequency. The cost: deterministic rules genuinely cannot express every nuance, and some legitimate work will be escalated unnecessarily. That is the correct direction to fail.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Prevented in Chapter 13; Chapter 20 develops it for security detectors.\par

\subsection{AP-12 — Ungated tool discovery and catalog dumping}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} A mature platform has many tools, and the agent cannot use what it cannot see. The tempting shortcut is “Every discovered tool and its full schema is placed into the prompt, often straight from an MCP server's advertised catalog.” It breaks down because discovery is being used as authorization. Selection gets harder as the catalog grows, because the model is choosing among near-neighbours it has no basis to distinguish. Schemas consume context that evidence should occupy. Worse, tool descriptions are untrusted text from a third party, so a malicious server can write a description engineered to attract calls — the agent is reading attacker-controlled instructions and treating them as a menu.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} A reporting agent discovers an administrative delete tool, because the catalog was filtered by connectivity rather than by the caller's authority.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} Registry → eligibility and policy evaluation → task-specific shortlist → schema validation at call time. Eligibility is computed from the subject, purpose, tenant, and risk class, never from what the server happens to advertise. Migrate in steps: Full MCP catalog becomes an approved tool slice bound to the run's capability envelope.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track tools exposed versus eligible versus actually used, selection-confusion rate, schema token overhead per run, and unauthorized discovery attempts. The cost: a narrow shortlist will sometimes omit a tool that would have helped. Make the omission observable so the shortlist can be improved deliberately, rather than widening it by default.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Previewed in Chapter 8; built in Chapters 19 and 22.\par

\section{Effects, retries and recovery}

These two cards are among the most expensive in the catalog, because they produce duplicate real-world effects — duplicate mortgage-document requests, duplicate payments, or duplicate emails to customers. Both stem from confusing the transport layer with the effect layer: a failed call is not a failed effect, and an unacknowledged call is not an absent one.\par

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/anti-retry-storm.pdf}{Figure 17-3 — Layered retries and timeout-as-failure versus one retry owner with an unknown-effect terminal}{anti-retry-storm}

\subsection{AP-04 — Retry everywhere}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Transient failures are real and common, and every layer in the stack offers a retry setting that looks like diligence. The tempting shortcut is “The SDK, the gateway, the workflow engine, the connector, and the tool each retry independently.” It breaks down because retries compose multiplicatively, not additively. Three layers at 3, 3, and 2 attempts produce eighteen calls for one logical operation, and each layer believes it made a modest choice. The amplification arrives precisely when a dependency is already degraded, converting a slow dependency into an outage. If any layer retries a non-idempotent call, the duplicates are permanent.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} A single mortgage evidence review produces eighteen calls to the payroll provider that verifies the second pay statement, and the provider rate-limits the lender for the rest of the day.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} One retry owner per failure class, with a shared attempt and deadline budget that every layer reads and no layer resets. Migrate in steps: Nested retries become classified failure → one schedule → terminal.\par

\BookNeedspace{312.0pt}

\BookSnake · AP-04 and its correction: one retry owner, one shared budget\par\nopagebreak

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
@dataclass
class AttemptBudget:
    remaining: int                 # shared by the run, decreasing, never reset

    def take(self) -> bool:
        if self.remaining <= 0:
            return False
        self.remaining -= 1
        return True


# Anti-pattern: each layer is independently "resilient". 3 x 3 x 2 = 18.
def build_naive(transport: Call) -> Call:
    client = with_retries(transport, attempts=3)
    gateway = with_retries(client, attempts=3)
    return with_retries(gateway, attempts=2)          # the workflow layer


# Correction: transport and gateway never retry. One owner retries transient
# failures only, from the run's budget; permanent fails fast, unknown reconciles.
def build_governed(transport: Call, budget: AttemptBudget) -> Call:
    def owner() -> str:
        last: Exception | None = None
        while budget.take():
            try:
                return transport()
            except TransientError as error:           # PermanentError and
                last = error                          # OutcomeUnknown propagate
        raise last or TransientError("attempt budget exhausted")
    return owner
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track attempts per logical operation, amplification factor against dependencies, duplicate effect keys, and deadline overruns caused by retry time. The cost: a single central policy is necessarily more conservative than a hand-tuned one per call site. Express the variation as failure classes rather than as per-layer settings.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Previewed in Chapter 3; built in Chapter 22.\par

\subsection{AP-05 — Timeout means failure}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} A response is lost after a request was dispatched, and the run has to decide what to do next. The tempting shortcut is “The call is marked failed and retried immediately.” It breaks down because a timeout describes the response, not the effect. The first call may have committed, may be committing, or may never have arrived, and the caller cannot distinguish these from the outside. Treating that state as failure asserts knowledge the system does not have, and the retry then creates the duplicate that the timeout only made possible.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} The mortgage document request is recorded twice: the first call committed just after the client gave up waiting.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} An explicit Unknown effect state that blocks retry until reconciliation settles it against the downstream system of record. Only proven absence permits a retry, and the retry reuses the same key; a request still executing stays Unknown, and an outcome that cannot be proven goes to InterventionRequired. Migrate in steps: timeout → failed → retry becomes timeout → Unknown → lookup → decide.\par

\BookNeedspace{284.4pt}

\BookSnake · AP-05 and its correction: a timeout leaves the effect Unknown\par\nopagebreak

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
# Anti-pattern: a timeout is treated as proof that nothing happened.
def request_document_naive(service: DocumentService, payload: dict[str, object]) -> str:
    try:
        return service.request(payload)
    except TimeoutError:
        return service.request(payload)       # the request may now exist twice


# Correction: Unknown blocks retry until the system of record decides.
def request_document(service: DocumentService, ledger: EffectLedger,
                     key: str, payload: dict[str, object]) -> str:
    def send() -> str:
        return service.request(payload, idempotency_key=key)

    try:
        return ledger.commit(key, payload, send)       # a timeout leaves it Unknown
    except EffectOutcomeUnknown:
        status, receipt = service.lookup(idempotency_key=key)
        if status == "committed":                      # it landed: adopt it
            ledger.reconcile(key, outcome="committed", evidence=receipt or "")
            return receipt or ""
        if status == "absent":                         # proven absent: same key
            ledger.reconcile(key, outcome="absent", evidence="downstream: absent")
            return ledger.commit(key, payload, send)
        if status == "unprovable":                     # InterventionRequired
            ledger.reconcile(key, outcome="unprovable", evidence="lookup inconclusive")
        raise                                          # executing: stays Unknown
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track count and age of unresolved unknown effects, duplicate idempotency keys observed downstream, and conflicts discovered after a timeout. The cost: unknown effects delay completion and need somewhere to go when reconciliation cannot resolve them. That queue is the price of not creating duplicate financial records.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Previewed in Chapters 1, 3, and 15; built in Chapter 22.\par

\section{Approval and acceptance}

Both cards here concern the moment a system decides that something is allowed, or that something is finished. In each, a cheap signal — a Boolean, a sentence of model output — was allowed to carry a decision that the enterprise will later be asked to defend. The correction is to make the decision an artifact: something with a hash, an owner, an expiry, and an independent check.\par

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/anti-approval-binding.pdf}{Figure 17-4 — Boolean consent versus proposal-bound, expiring approval}{anti-approval-binding}

\subsection{AP-06 — Boolean or conversational approval}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Humans must approve protected actions, and a chat interface makes approval feel natural. The tempting shortcut is “A Boolean flag or a chat message — “yes, go ahead” — approves whatever the arguments happen to be at execution time.” It breaks down because approval is being bound to a conversation rather than to an effect. Between consent and commit, the amount, the recipient, the policy version, the acting workload, or the underlying record can all change, and nothing detects it. The approver reviewed a description; the system executes a payload. A Boolean also cannot express scope or expiry, so a single “yes” silently approves later, larger, and unrelated actions.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} An approval given to submit the evidence package for case version 3 is reused after the case moved to version 5 and the package gained a document nobody reviewed.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} An immutable proposal, hashed over the exact effect, with an ApprovalDecision bound to that hash, scoped to one action, and carrying an expiry. Revalidate policy and resource version immediately before commit, and key the commit by the logical effect, not by the proposal hash. Migrate in steps: approved=true becomes proposal hash → ApprovalDecision → revalidate → commit.\par

\BookNeedspace{183.2pt}

\BookSnake · AP-06 and its correction: the approval covers this exact proposal\par\nopagebreak

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
# Anti-pattern: consent is a flag on the run, detached from what executes.
def submit_naive(run: Run, underwriting: Underwriting) -> str | None:
    if run.approved:
        return underwriting.submit_evidence_package(case_id=run.case_id)
    return None


# Correction: the decision covers this exact proposal, now, against the
# current policy and case version, immediately before commit.
def submit(proposal: ApprovalRequest, decision: ApprovalDecision,
           underwriting: Underwriting, *, now: datetime, case_version: int) -> str:
    verify_approval(proposal, decision, now=now, actual_resource_version=case_version)
    return underwriting.submit_evidence_package(
        **proposal.arguments,
        idempotency_key=proposal.logical_effect_key,   # business intent, not the hash
    )
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track proposal-hash mismatches at commit, approval reuse attempts, expiry rate, and incidents occurring after a recorded decision. The cost: benign changes force reapproval, which reviewers experience as friction. Tune by making the diff visible — showing what changed is cheaper than widening the approval.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Prevented in Chapter 14.\par

\subsection{AP-07 — Model says done}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} A run needs a completion signal, and the model produces a confident, well-formed one for free. The tempting shortcut is “The model's assertion sets the run to success.” It breaks down because the model is reporting on its own work using the same faculty that produced it. A missing evidence artifact, a skipped approval, an uncommitted effect, or an unmet obligation all present as success, because none of them changes what the model believes it did. Failures therefore surface downstream — in reconciliation, in audit, or in a customer complaint — long after the trace has aged out.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} The evidence review reports complete while the second pay statement's verification is stale, because the model saw a verification result in context and did not evaluate its freshness.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} An independent, executable acceptance check over authoritative state returns an AcceptanceDecision—accepted, rejected with typed defects, or escalated (Chapter 13); only an accepted decision lets the run reach Succeeded (Chapter 15). While an effect is Unknown or required input is missing, acceptance is not attempted. The model may propose completion; it may not declare it. Migrate in steps: Model text → success becomes acceptance engine → AcceptanceDecision → run state.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track false-completion rate found by audit, reopen rate, and runs closed with missing artifacts or uncommitted effects. The cost: executable acceptance criteria need domain ownership and maintenance. Without them the completion signal has no author, which is worse and merely less visible.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Prevented in Chapters 2, 13, and 15.\par

\section{Multi-agent collaboration}

\subsection{AP-08 — Multi-agent chat as workflow}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Specialists need to coordinate, and a shared conversation is the fastest coordination mechanism to build. The tempting shortcut is “Free-form messages between agents own state, work assignment, deadlines, and completion.” It breaks down because a conversation has no notion of ownership, so two agents can both believe the other is acting — or both act. It has no join semantics, so nothing knows when parallel work has finished. Cancellation cannot propagate, because there is no structure to propagate through, and artifacts produced mid-conversation are lost when the window compacts. The system behaves like a meeting with no chair and no minutes.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} The income-verification and policy specialists each wait for the other's artifact. The run sits open for three days and neither agent is wrong.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} A typed TaskEnvelope with exactly one accountable owner, explicit child tasks, artifacts stored by reference, and a deterministic join evaluated by the coordinator rather than inferred from messages. Migrate in steps: Chat mesh becomes supervisor workflow plus task service.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track orphaned tasks, conflicting concurrent effects, delegation depth, join wait age, and effects landing after a run was considered complete. The cost: formal task envelopes reduce the fluidity that makes agent conversations appealing to demonstrate. The fluidity is what fails in production.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Built in Chapter 18.\par

\section{Observability and evidence}

\subsection{AP-14 — Telemetry without causal or effect lineage}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Teams instrument what they own, and each component's dashboard looks healthy. The tempting shortcut is “Logs, metrics, and traces cannot be joined to versions, policy decisions, effects, acceptance, or business outcome.” It breaks down because without a join key, every measurement is local. A component can improve on its own metric while degrading the outcome, and nothing in the telemetry can express the relationship. Cost cannot be attributed to a tenant, a release, or a decision, so optimization targets whatever is easiest to measure. When an incident happens, the investigation has to reconstruct causality by hand from timestamps.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} A retrieval cache raises its hit rate and cuts latency, while stale-evidence rejections at acceptance double. Both facts are true and neither dashboard contains both.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} One causal run trace joining release version, context manifest, model and policy decisions, tool effects, acceptance decision, business outcome, and cost. Migrate in steps: Separate dashboards become one correlated trace.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track percentage of runs joinable to a business outcome, spans missing parent or version identifiers, and unattributed spend. The cost: correlation requires disciplined identifier propagation across every component and raises the governance burden on telemetry, including redaction. Retrofitting it after an incident costs considerably more.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Built in Chapter 24.\par

\section{Platform, release and operations}

Both cards describe the same instinct — change should be fast — applied where it removes the ability to explain, reproduce, or undo what happened. Speed is not the problem; unversioned speed is.\par

\subsection{AP-15 — Model or provider swap as configuration}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} Teams want better cost or quality, and the routing layer makes the change a one-line edit. The tempting shortcut is “A model alias is repointed without behavioral validation, because no application code changed.” It breaks down because the model is the behavior. Tool-calling reliability, schema adherence, refusal boundaries, safety posture, latency distribution, and cost per task all shift together, and they shift unevenly across slices — a change that is neutral in aggregate can be severe for one language, one document type, or one tenant. Because no release event was recorded, the resulting regression has no obvious cause and cannot be rolled back precisely.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} A cheaper model drops tool arguments for one language only. Aggregate evaluation stays green; that market's completion rate halves.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} A versioned ChangeBundle (the versioned change proposal that Chapter 25 evaluates and Chapter 27 promotes) carrying evaluation across slices, security testing, load testing, canary exposure, and a named rollback target. Migrate in steps: Edit alias becomes immutable route policy → replay → approval → canary.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track schema and tool-call regression rate, refusal-rate shift, per-slice quality drift, and fallback-route activation. The cost: validation slows experimentation, so make the fast path cheap: pre-built replay sets and automated slice reports, rather than an approval meeting.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Previewed in Chapters 3 and 7; built in Chapters 25 and 27.\par

\subsection{AP-16 — In-place change and test after release}

\textbf{How this failure starts.} A production issue needs a fix now, and the console offers an edit box. The tempting shortcut is “Prompts, Skills, tool definitions, policy, and routes are mutated directly in production.” It breaks down because a run can no longer be reproduced, because the artifacts it used no longer exist in the form it used them. Attribution fails, since two runs a minute apart may have used different behavior with no record of the boundary. Rollback becomes guesswork about what the previous state was. And the edit itself is untested by construction — the test is the production traffic.\par

\textbf{What it looks like in practice.} A prompt edit intended to reduce verbosity increases data leakage. Neither the previous prompt nor the exact leak conditions can be reconstructed.\par

\textbf{How to replace it.} Immutable artifacts, a ReleaseManifest that pins everything a run resolves, pre-promotion tests, signed promotion, canary exposure, and a rollback target that is verified rather than assumed. Migrate in steps: Mutable console becomes build → validate → approve → promote → monitor.\par

\textbf{How to know the repair worked.} Track runs executing unversioned artifacts, out-of-band edit events, and rollback rehearsal failures. The cost: immutability needs genuinely good tooling, or people will route around it under pressure. If the emergency path is slower than the console, the console wins — so make the fast path immutable rather than making immutability optional.\par

\textbf{Prevented in / Built in.} Previewed in Chapters 7 and 8; built in Chapter 27.\par

\section{Where this leads next}

Several of these failures are corrected by mechanisms the rest of the book builds. Chapter 18 starts with AP-08 and AP-11: how specialists can share work with one owner per task, narrowed authority, explicit joins, and a bounded task tree.\par

\BookPartPage{V}{WORKING TOGETHER ACROSS BOUNDARIES}{Once a system has more than one worker—or reaches beyond its own process—the conversation becomes a distributed protocol. This Part makes the ownership, authority, cancellation, and artifact rules explicit for internal specialists and external peers alike. It also keeps a useful distinction in view: a protocol can move a message without granting permission to act.}

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-18-coordinate-specialists-without-losing-control.png}

\BookChapter{18}{Coordinate Specialists Without Losing Control}

\begin{bookquote}

\textbf{Evidence context:} Public multi-agent implementation reports describe supervisor/specialist designs and substantial token/context trade-offs; these observations justify explicit topology and cost tests rather than universal multi-agent adoption. \textbf{\BookCitation{R28}\BookCitation{R33}}

\end{bookquote}

\begin{bookcallout}[Where The Mortgage Case Stands]
Document extraction, income verification, property review, and policy specialists can run concurrently, but a plausible multi-agent conversation is not a coordination protocol. Harborlight defines durable tasks, one owner per task, narrowed authority, explicit joins, and artifact contracts.
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Give one component ownership of each task, derive narrower child authority, and define explicit join and cancellation semantics.
\end{bookcallout}

\section{Why more agents do not automatically make a better system}

Multiple agents can shorten a workflow, but conversation alone does not tell you who owns a task, who may cancel it, or which result wins when specialists disagree. Chapter 17 showed the two ways this goes wrong: chat used as the workflow (AP-08) and budgets that reset at every hop (AP-11). This chapter builds the replacement: a task tree with one owner per task, narrowing authority, explicit joins, and durable cancellation.\par

\section{What every collaboration must make explicit}

Every additional agent introduces another scheduler, context boundary, identity, budget, and failure mode. That cost is justified when a specialist has meaningfully different expertise, data access, tools, ownership, latency, or scaling needs. It is not justified merely because a multi-agent conversation looks modular. The collaboration contract makes the cost visible by requiring each task to have one owner, one capability envelope, explicit artifacts, and a rule for joining or canceling work.\par

\BookNeedspace{51.4pt}

\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont

\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
Task Service and supervisor workflow & parent\slash{}child authority and artifact adoption & task tree · specialist queue & one child task & TaskEnvelope · CapabilityEnvelope · JoinPolicy \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Treat collaboration as durable work, not conversation}

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/supervisor-worker.pdf}{Figure 18-1 — Supervisor/worker: narrowed child grants, an explicit fan-in contract, and durable cancellation}{supervisor-worker}

Harborlight’s supervisor owns the evidence-package outcome and creates durable child tasks for document extraction, income verification, property review, and policy explanation. The extraction child runs Chapter 5's document ingestion as a capability and returns a KnowledgeArtifact, not free text. Each child receives only the capability, evidence, budget, deadline, and cancellation scope it needs.\par

Each child's authority is a \emph{capability envelope}: the maximum actions, resources, purposes, data classes, regions, risk, cost, delegation depth, and lifetime it may use. The Task Service derives it from the parent's envelope by intersection and refuses any child envelope that is not a provable subset. Chapter 21 defines the envelope and gives the subset proof.\par

Fan-in is explicit: wait for all, quorum, first valid, or fallback. Artifact conflicts use deterministic precedence, source freshness, verification status (Chapter 5), confidence thresholds, or human resolution. Free-form conversation may help agents explain results, but it cannot replace task status, ownership, artifact, or effect records.\par

Nested delegation multiplies cost and risk. The Task Service caps depth, children, concurrency, total turns, total tokens, external calls, and wall-clock time across the entire task tree (the AP-11 correction). Parent cancellation propagates as durable intent; a child with an executing mutation must reconcile before acknowledging final cancellation (Chapter 22 § A reliable protocol for changing the outside world).\par

\section{Choose a topology—and define how the work ends}

Central coordination is easier to govern but can bottleneck; decentralized collaboration may widen coverage at the cost of determinism, auditability, and cost control. Routing and handoff keep the meanings from Chapter 11: routing chooses a worker; handoff transfers accountability and completes only when the recipient acknowledges.\par

\BookNeedspace{122.6pt}

\subsection{Topology decision table}

\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Pattern} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Use when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Avoid when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Control model} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Pattern} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Use when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Avoid when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Control model} \\
\endhead
One model call + retrieval & Answer is bounded and no external side effect is needed & Task requires iterative environmental feedback & Request\slash{}response \\*
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Single agent loop & Open-ended steps share the same permissions, context, and owner & Multiple teams or security zones must be isolated & One agent, bounded tool loop \\*
Deterministic workflow & Steps and invariants are known & Discovery or decomposition cannot be anticipated & Code\slash{}state machine owns routing \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Router + specialists & Inputs fall into stable classes with distinct prompts\slash{}tools & Classification is unstable or branches share nearly all context & Deterministic or model router with confidence threshold \\
Manager + agents as tools & One coordinator must synthesize a final answer & Specialist should own the user relationship & Parent retains task ownership \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Handoff & A specialist must take over the next user-facing branch & The work is a hidden bounded subtask & Ownership transfers only when the recipient accepts \\
Hierarchical fan-out\slash{}fan-in & Subtasks are independent and benefit from parallelism & Shared mutable context or tight ordering dominates & Coordinator sets fan-out and aggregation contract \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Peer swarm \slash{} group chat & Research exploration where diversity is worth the cost & Regulated decisions, side effects, or strict SLOs & Experimental; hard budgets and sandbox only \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{Recommended orchestration rules}

\begin{itemize}

\item \textbf{Creation:} instantiate agents from an immutable AgentSpec; never construct production instructions and permissions ad hoc in a prompt.

\item \textbf{Delegation:} create a child task carrying a TaskEnvelope (§ Durable child-task contract).

\item \textbf{Handoff:} follow Chapter 11's acknowledged transfer. The Ownership Service records it as an OwnershipTransfer with a narrowed capability envelope; the prior owner remains accountable until the recipient accepts and is kept as history afterward, not as a competing owner.

\item \textbf{Coordination:} use a workflow node, not a group chat, to decide readiness, joins, retries, cancellation, and aggregation.

\item \textbf{Shared state:} store it in a concurrency-controlled state service. Agents receive snapshots and return proposed patches with expected versions.

\item \textbf{Termination:} terminal transitions follow the task lifecycle below; the model may recommend completion but cannot make the transition.

\item \textbf{Plan changes:} use Chapter 12's revision protocol; new nodes are validated against the original capability envelope, and materially broader scope needs new approval.

\end{itemize}

\BookNeedspace{176.2pt}

\subsection{Task lifecycle}

\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Entered when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Who may move it on} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Entered when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Who may move it on} \\
\endhead
Created\slash{}Dispatched & The Task Service persists the TaskEnvelope under its idempotency key and queues it for the receiver & The receiver accepts or rejects; the parent may cancel \\*
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Accepted\slash{}Running & The receiver calls \lstinline[style=bookinline,basicstyle=\ttfamily\color{BookNavyTwo}]!:accept! with the expected version; a child workflow starts (Chapter 15) & The owner, by completing, failing, or requesting input \\*
NeedsInput & The owner needs a clarification, document, or approval it cannot obtain itself & The parent or user supplies it; the deadline moves it to Expired \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
CompletedPendingValidation & The owner submits artifacts through \lstinline[style=bookinline,basicstyle=\ttfamily\color{BookNavyTwo}]!:complete!; they stay quarantined & The Task Service, after schema, provenance, and acceptance checks \\
Succeeded & Validation passes and the parent's join adopts the artifacts (Chapter 15 § Terminal correctness) & No one; terminal \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
CancelRequested → Canceled & The parent persists cancellation intent; Canceled only after any executing mutation reconciles & The Task Service, once children acknowledge and effects settle \\
Failed \slash{} Expired & A typed failure (including the receiver's rejection) or a passed deadline & No one; terminal, and the parent applies its join policy \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
InterventionRequired & An outcome no rule can settle; an intervention task opens (Chapter 14) & The intervention task's response; non-terminal until it resolves the task (Chapter 15 § Terminal correctness) \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

The receiver may reject, accept, request input, produce partial artifacts, complete, fail, or acknowledge cancellation. Terminal states reuse Chapter 15's run terminal names, and a terminal task accepts no new work; follow-up is a new task. Cancellation propagates downward by default; compensation does not propagate automatically and follows domain policy (Chapter 22).\par

\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Entity} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required fields and invariants} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Entity} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required fields and invariants} \\
\endhead
AgentVersion & (tenant\_id, agent\_id, version), digest, instructions digest, tool allowlist, schemas, owner, risk tier, lifecycle state; immutable after release \\*
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Task & task ID, tenant\slash{}run\slash{}parent IDs, logical key, owner agent\slash{}version, state (§ Task lifecycle), goal, input\slash{}output schema refs, deadline, priority, current revision; unique (tenant\_id, run\_id, logical\_key) \\*

\rowcolor{BookLight}
TaskArtifact & task ID, role (input\slash{}partial\slash{}output\slash{}evidence), immutable artifact URI\slash{}hash, schema, producer, classification \\

\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{State, consistency, and scale}

\begin{itemize}

\item Partition by tenant + run so a run's tasks are shard-local; let the run's durable workflow serialize its control decisions; use strongly consistent transactions for task creation, owner transfer, terminal transition, and budget reservation, and eventually consistent projections for the graph and analytics.

\item Dispatch by capability + region + risk class (Chapter 22) + priority. A receiver queue contains task references, not full sensitive context; workers fetch authorized artifacts after assignment.

\item Enforce manifest-level defaults such as max\_delegation\_depth, max\_children\_per\_task, max\_parallel\_children, and max\_plan\_revisions; risk policy may lower them per run.

\item Mitigate “hot runs” by batching status events, limiting concurrent ready nodes, and applying per-run semaphores. A single run should not require more coordinator throughput than its bounded fan-out permits.

\item Idempotent task creation and completion make queue redelivery safe. Task terminal state plus accepted artifact hash is immutable; corrections create a new revision/task rather than reopening history.

\end{itemize}

\section{Supervisor/Worker at a glance}

\subsection{How the pattern works}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Decompose only along meaningful specialist boundaries.

\item Create durable TaskEnvelopes and child capability envelopes.

\item Schedule independently with deadlines and cancellation lineage.

\item Collect artifacts under explicit join semantics and adopt them only after schema, provenance, quality, and policy validation.

\item Resolve conflicts and accept the parent outcome.

\end{enumerate}

\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Use when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Do not use when} \\
\endhead
Use when specialists differ in tools, data, trust, cost, SLO, ownership, or deployment and parallel work helps. & Avoid when one agent with tools is simpler, context is inseparable, or coordination cost exceeds specialization gain, or when free-form conversation would be the only coordination record. \\
\end{longtable}

\section{A sensible place to start}

Start with the one contract every child task carries.\par

\subsection{Durable child-task contract}

A TaskEnvelope carries eight field groups:\par

\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-2\tabcolsep)/2\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-2\tabcolsep)/2\relax}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Field group} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Minimum contents} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Field group} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Minimum contents} \\
\endhead
Identity & task\_id, parent\_run\_id, tenant, requester subject, caller workload, receiving agent\slash{}version \\*
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Goal & Bounded objective, non-goals, acceptance criteria, priority, deadline \\*
Data & Immutable input artifact references, content hashes, provenance, classification, permitted purpose \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Contract & Input\slash{}output schemas, artifact media types, status model (§ Task lifecycle) \\
Authority & Capability envelope reference (\lstinline[style=bookinline,basicstyle=\ttfamily\color{BookNavyTwo}]!capability_grant_id!), denied operations, maximum delegation depth, expiry \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Budgets & Tokens, currency, wall time, steps, tool calls, parallelism, data-egress limit \\
Reliability & Idempotency key, dependencies, retry ownership, heartbeat, cancel semantics, compensation owner \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Observability & Trace\slash{}span context, causation\slash{}correlation IDs, sampling class, audit references \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

The model below carries one field or more from each group, and the Task Service enforces the three rules that make delegation safe: a reused idempotency key returns the original task, a child's delegation depth and budget come out of its parent's, and the capability-envelope subset check (Chapter 21) runs before any child exists.\par

\BookNeedspace{109.6pt}

\BookSnake · Provider-neutral agent-step and platform contracts\par\nopagebreak

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
class TaskEnvelope(BaseModel):
    """The one child-task contract (Chapter 18), grouped as the field-group table."""

    model_config = ConfigDict(frozen=True)

    # Identity
    task_id: str
    run_id: str
    parent_task_id: str | None = None
    tenant_id: str
    subject_id: str
    owner_agent: str  # receiving agent@version
    # Goal
    goal: str
    acceptance_contract: str
    deadline: datetime
    # Data and contract
    input_artifact_ids: tuple[str, ...] = ()
    output_schema: str
    # Authority: the capability envelope this task may use (Chapter 21)
    capability_grant_id: str
    delegation_depth: int = Field(ge=0)  # further delegation levels allowed
    # Budgets
    maximum_cost_usd: Decimal = Field(ge=0)
    # Reliability
    idempotency_key: str
    depends_on: tuple[str, ...] = ()
    cancellation_parent_id: str | None = None
    state_version: int = 0
    # Observability
    trace_context: str  # W3C traceparent


SubsetCheck = Callable[[str, str], bool]  # (parent grant, child grant) -> provably narrower?


class TaskConflict(Exception):
    """An idempotency key was reused for a different task."""


class TaskService:
    def __init__(self, is_subset: SubsetCheck) -> None:
        self._is_subset = is_subset
        self._by_key: dict[tuple[str, str], TaskEnvelope] = {}
        self._remaining: dict[str, Decimal] = {}

    def create_child(self, parent: TaskEnvelope, child: TaskEnvelope) -> TaskEnvelope:
        existing = self._by_key.get((child.run_id, child.idempotency_key))
        if existing is not None:
            if existing != child:
                raise TaskConflict(child.idempotency_key)
            return existing  # redelivery: same task, no second withdrawal
        if (child.run_id, child.parent_task_id) != (parent.run_id, parent.task_id):
            raise ValueError("child must name its parent task and run")
        if (child.tenant_id, child.subject_id) != (parent.tenant_id, parent.subject_id):
            raise PermissionError("tenant and subject cannot change")
        if child.delegation_depth >= parent.delegation_depth:
            raise PermissionError("delegation depth was not reduced")
        if child.deadline > parent.deadline:
            raise PermissionError("child deadline exceeds the parent's")
        remaining = self._remaining.get(parent.task_id, parent.maximum_cost_usd)
        if child.maximum_cost_usd > remaining:
            raise PermissionError("budget exceeds the parent's remaining budget")
        if not self._is_subset(parent.capability_grant_id, child.capability_grant_id):
            raise PermissionError("capability envelope is not a subset of the parent's")
        self._remaining[parent.task_id] = remaining - child.maximum_cost_usd
        self._by_key[(child.run_id, child.idempotency_key)] = child
        return child
\end{lstlisting}

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Build the durable task tree and identify the current owner of every node.

\item Verify each child grant is a subset of the parent grant (the subset proof in Chapter 21 § A sensible place to start).

\item Inspect join semantics, missing artifacts, duplicates, and conflicts.

\end{enumerate}

If chat is the coordination record or each child gets fresh limits, see AP-08 and AP-11 in Chapter 17.\par

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Another agent exists only when expertise, tools, data, trust, ownership, SLO, or scaling truly differs.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Delegation creates a typed durable task with one current owner.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Child authority is provably narrower than parent authority.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Joins define all, quorum, first-valid, or fallback semantics and conflict policy.\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Design supervisor/worker fan-out with quorum and a deterministic conflict rule.

\item Model cascading cancellation across two local agents and one remote task.

\end{enumerate}

\BookNeedspace{202.7pt}

\section{Questions to bring to design review}

\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-2\tabcolsep)/2\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-2\tabcolsep)/2\relax}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endhead
When is a multi-agent system justified? & When meaningful boundaries differ—domain, tools, data access, owner, deployment, SLO, or cost—not merely because multiple personas sound sophisticated. \\*
\rowcolor{BookLight}
What must happen before a parent cancellation is reported complete? & Cancellation is persisted as durable intent and propagated to every child; children with executing mutations reconcile before acknowledging; the API distinguishes requested, acknowledged, and completed. \\*
How do you prevent delegation from escalating privilege? & Derive child authority by intersection; narrow action, resource, data, time, budget, and depth; reauthorize each invocation; issue scoped last-hop credentials. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
How should fan-in be implemented? & Persist child states and artifacts, wait for an explicit join condition, then validate schemas, provenance, conflicts, quality, and policy deterministically. \\
How do you keep a task tree inside its budget? & Children withdraw from the parent's remaining budget rather than receive fresh limits; depth, children, concurrency, and plan revisions are capped by manifest defaults that risk policy may lower; per-run semaphores bound ready nodes. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Where this leads next}

Inside one platform, the Task Service enforces these rules directly. Chapter 19 carries the same ownership and narrowing rules to tools and agents outside the platform, where a protocol moves the message but not the trust.\par

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-19-crossing-system-boundaries-mcp-a2a-and-local-control.png}

\BookChapter{19}{Crossing System Boundaries: MCP, A2A, and Local Control}

\begin{bookquote}

\textbf{Evidence context:} MCP 2026-07-28 defines a stateless, self-contained protocol core for resources, prompts, and tools, while A2A v1.0.1 defines agent cards and task/message/artifact exchange. Neither protocol transfers application authorization or business acceptance automatically. \textbf{\BookCitation{R05}\BookCitation{R06}\BookCitation{R07}}

\end{bookquote}

\begin{bookcallout}[Where The Mortgage Case Stands]
Harborlight consumes a third-party income-verification MCP server, and the property-review specialist from Chapter 18 is now a partner-operated agent reached through A2A. Protocol conformance makes messages interoperable; it does not make the endpoint trusted or the artifact safe to adopt.
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Terminate MCP and A2A at governed local gateways; verify endpoints, artifacts, authority, quotas, and effect semantics locally.
\end{bookcallout}

\section{Why interoperability does not transfer trust}

External protocols make it easier to discover and exchange work with tools and agents outside your boundary. They do not make those endpoints trustworthy, or their artifacts safe to adopt. An MCP server can describe a tool accurately and still be unauthorized for this tenant; an A2A peer can accept a task and still lack the evidence or identity needed to act. Model providers are a third external boundary; the LLM Gateway that terminates it sits in the Gateways and integration plane (Chapter 4), and model routing, which filters by policy before it optimizes, is treated as a cost decision in Chapter 28 (Figure 28-2).\par

\BookNeedspace{51.4pt}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-8\tabcolsep)/5\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-8\tabcolsep)/5\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-8\tabcolsep)/5\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-8\tabcolsep)/5\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-8\tabcolsep)/5\relax}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
MCP and A2A Gateways & local trust domain to external endpoint or peer & endpoint · peer · tenant quota & one remote call or task & ServerManifest · FederatedTask · RemoteArtifact \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Two protocols, two responsibilities}

MCP and A2A are routinely discussed as alternatives. They are not, and choosing between them is usually the wrong question — they standardize different things, and a mature system runs both.\par

\BookNeedspace{153.1pt}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries  & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{MCP} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{A2A} \\
Standardizes & How a host discovers and invokes \textbf{tools, resources, and prompts} on a server & How peers collaborate around a \textbf{task}: messages, status, streaming, artifacts, cancellation \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Unit of exchange & A tool call and its result & A task with a lifecycle \\
Who owns the work & The caller, throughout & Transfers to the peer, which reports status back \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Typical use & “Read this record for me” & “Own this sub-goal and tell me when it is done” \\
What it does \textbf{not} decide & Whether the caller may invoke the tool & Whether the peer may be trusted with the task \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

That last row is the one that matters: neither protocol carries business authorization, so each terminates at a gateway that supplies the decision the protocol omits.\par

The MCP Gateway resolves a pinned ServerManifest, authenticates the endpoint, filters tools to the caller's eligible set, validates schemas, and constrains egress. It validates audience-bound tokens and never passes an inbound token upstream. A mutating MCP call then continues to the Tool Gateway (Chapter 1), which reauthorizes the exact resource, enforces idempotency, and records the effect receipt; the MCP Gateway decides whether the server and tool may be used, not whether the effect may happen.\par

The A2A Gateway is the agent-to-agent control point in Chapter 1's gateway table: it decides whether this external peer may receive or return this task, and it terminates the protocol; inside the platform the same decision is made by the Task Service (Chapter 18). It verifies peer identity and trust profile, translates Chapter 18's TaskEnvelope into an A2A task (adding the remote task and context IDs, peer and card digest, and protocol version, recorded in FederatedTask), persists status, enforces deadlines and quotas, and quarantines returned artifacts until local adoption.\par

\subsection{Registry model}

A ServerManifest (MCP) or RemoteAgent entry (A2A) contains owner, endpoint, protocol version and profile, authentication method, trust material, schemas, supported capabilities, data classifications, regions, risk, rate limits, health, release aliases, evaluation evidence, and revocation state. It is pinned by digest; a changed manifest is a new version that must pass policy before use. The gateway pins one MCP protocol revision per server and negotiates explicitly; Tasks and Skills over MCP are optional extensions, so durable ownership never depends on them. Discovery never substitutes for authorization (Chapter 8 § Discovery is not permission; AP-12). An operational registry product can host these entries (Appendix D notes the options and their maturity), but it holds metadata, not authorization.\par

\BookNeedspace{176.5pt}

\section{Delegate work across a trust boundary}

\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-2\tabcolsep)/2\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-2\tabcolsep)/2\relax}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required analysis} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Design conclusion} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required analysis} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Design conclusion} \\
\endhead
Problem & Independent agents need to discover capabilities, delegate work, exchange artifacts, propagate failure, and agree on ownership without exposing internal prompts or state. \\*
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Why it is difficult & Natural-language messages are ambiguous; remote agents have separate identities and SLOs; asynchronous tasks outlive connections; protocol interoperability does not guarantee shared authorization or semantics. \\
Recommended architecture & For agents under one platform, use workflow-native child tasks. Across deployment or vendor boundaries, use the A2A Gateway with typed task\slash{}artifact envelopes and authenticated async operations. Use MCP for tools\slash{}resources. Never use conversation alone as the durable task record. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Trade-offs & Standard protocols reduce integration cost but cover transport more than governance. A gateway may reduce protocol features, yet it supplies policy, tenant binding, quotas, schema validation, and audit. \\
Failure scenarios & Agent advertises a misleading capability; task status is lost; result schema differs; duplicate delegation starts duplicate work; parent cancels but child continues; remote agent requests broader rights. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\BookNeedspace{255.0pt}

\subsection{Protocol roles}

\BookNeedspace{217.0pt}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Mechanism} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Best use} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Not sufficient for} \\
Direct function call & Low-latency, same-process specialist & Independent lifecycle, failure isolation, cross-team deployment \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Durable child workflow & Same trust domain, long-running task with strong parent\slash{}child semantics & Cross-vendor discovery and open interoperability \\
Event bus & Facts, triggers, status distribution, loose coupling & Request ownership, interactive clarification, synchronous results \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
MCP & Tools, prompts, and resources exposed to an agent & Full multi-agent task lifecycle and enterprise authorization policy \\
A2A & Agent discovery, messages, stateful tasks, streaming\slash{}push updates, artifacts & Internal durable execution, shared business transactions, policy authority \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Shared blackboard & Experimental collaboration and broad exploration & Transactional ownership, tenant isolation, deterministic conflict resolution \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

A2A’s task/message/artifact model and async operations are useful at remote-agent boundaries. Its agent card is discovery metadata—not proof of trust. Pin the card version or digest, verify issuer and endpoint, and filter advertised skills through enterprise policy—an advertised A2A skill is discovery metadata, not a certified Skill in the sense of Chapter 8. Across trust domains, never share mutable state: exchange immutable artifacts or versioned patches under optimistic concurrency.\par

\section{A workable implementation}

\subsection{Who should own each decision}

The Agent Directory stores validated cards and endpoint metadata for remote agents alongside the Agent Registry's local versions (Chapter 18), and \lstinline[style=bookinline]!agents:resolve! queries both; the Peer Trust Registry stores organizational trust and allowed purposes; the A2A Gateway terminates authentication and protocol bindings; the Task Bridge maps remote tasks to internal child workflows; the Artifact Broker exchanges immutable references; the Subscription Dispatcher handles push/status delivery; and the Trace Bridge links remote and local causal graphs.\par

\BookNeedspace{151.1pt}

\subsection{The contracts between components}

\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{API} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Purpose and key semantics} \\

\rowcolor{BookLight}

\rowcolor{BookLight}

\rowcolor{BookLight}
POST \slash{}v1\slash{}a2a\slash{}callbacks & Authenticated ingress for peer status\slash{}artifact events with source+event ID deduplication \\

At an open boundary, expose the standard A2A agent-card and task/message operations. Internally, normalize every binding to the platform’s task state, identity, artifact, error, and audit contracts.\par

\subsection{State mapping and ownership}

Local states are the task lifecycle of Chapter 18; the gateway maps each remote state of the pinned A2A 1.0.1 specification onto one of them.\par

\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}Y{\dimexpr(\linewidth-4\tabcolsep)/3\relax}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Local state} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{A2A task state} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Parent behavior} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Local state} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{A2A task state} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Parent behavior} \\
\endhead
Created\slash{}Dispatched & TASK\_STATE\_SUBMITTED & Retry dispatch only with same idempotency key \\*
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Accepted\slash{}Running & TASK\_STATE\_WORKING & Monitor heartbeat and deadline \\*
NeedsInput & TASK\_STATE\_INPUT\_REQUIRED & Route a typed question to parent\slash{}user; budget clock follows policy \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
NeedsInput & TASK\_STATE\_AUTH\_REQUIRED & Route to the credential or approval path, not the user prompt \\
CompletedPendingValidation & TASK\_STATE\_COMPLETED & Quarantine artifacts until schema, provenance, malware, policy, and acceptance checks pass \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Succeeded & No remote state; local adoption committed through \lstinline[style=bookinline,basicstyle=\ttfamily\color{BookNavyTwo}]!:adoptResult! & Parent may satisfy dependency \\
CancelRequested & CancelTask sent; no terminal state yet & Stop issuing new authority; continue monitoring possible effects \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Canceled & TASK\_STATE\_CANCELED & Close only after any possible effect reconciles \\
Failed & TASK\_STATE\_FAILED or TASK\_STATE\_REJECTED & Apply parent join policy \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Expired & Deadline passed & Apply parent join policy; never resubmit automatically \\
InterventionRequired & Remote outcome ambiguous & Open an intervention task and hold dependent work until its response; never resubmit automatically \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{Scalability, federation, and failure isolation}

\begin{itemize}

\item Partition task-relay state by tenant + peer domain + task ID. Serialize updates for one federated task while scaling across tasks and peers.

\item Prefer asynchronous callbacks or streaming for progress; poll only as a bounded fallback with exponential backoff. Persist callbacks before acknowledgment.

\item Enforce per-peer and per-skill rate, concurrency, byte-egress, and cost budgets. Circuit-break a misbehaving peer without disabling unrelated local agents.

\item Exchange large or sensitive data by short-lived, audience-bound artifact references. Validate recipient, region, classification, and purpose before issuing each reference.

\item Carry W3C trace context when supported and create span links when the remote system returns a different trace. Never rely on a remote trace as the enterprise audit record.

\item Keep peer card/configuration reads cached by signed digest, but re-evaluate authorization and trust policy per task/message. Card changes cannot alter an in-flight task’s pinned contract.

\item Bound remote fan-out at the parent. A peer may not recursively delegate unless the task grant explicitly permits a depth and the resulting chain remains visible.

\item Treat missed heartbeat, callback failure, cancellation ambiguity, and artifact validation failure as distinct states; do not automatically resubmit a potentially active remote task.

\end{itemize}
